 189: | `--speed <F>` | Multiply WPM by a factor (e.g. `2.0` to finish quickly) |
 190: | `--no-typos` | Disable typo simulation for this resume |
 191: | `--strict` | No CLI nav fallback; exit on first error |
 192: 
 193: ### `ghostwriter check`
 194: 
 195: Verify the environment before starting a long run.
 196: 
 197: ```
 198: ghostwriter check [--repo <URL>] [--api] [--api-key <KEY>]
 199: ```
 200: 
 201: Checks: VSCode CLI on PATH, screen capture, Accessibility permission, optional network+git, optional API key validity.
 202: 
 203: ### `ghostwriter plan`
 204: 
 205: Generate a `DevelopmentScript` without recording anything.
 206: 
 207: ```
 208: ghostwriter plan --repo <URL> [--save <FILE>] [--verbose]
 209: ```
 210: 
 211: Useful for reviewing what ghostwriter will do, editing scripts before running, or generating scripts in CI for offline playback.
 212: 
 213: ---
 214: 
 215: ## Session file schema (reference)
 216: 
 217: ```toml
 218: [session]
 219: id                  = "<6-char hex>"       # log correlation ID
 220: repo                = "<url or path>"      # original --repo value
 221: dest                = "<absolute path>"    # recording destination
 222: started_at          = "<RFC-3339>"
 223: ghostwriter_version = "<semver>"
 224: 
 225: [run]
 226: seed            = <u64 or absent>          # RNG seed; absent = entropy-seeded
 227: wpm_effective   = <u32>                    # WPM after --speed multiplier applied
 228: no_typos        = <bool>
 229: script_path     = "<absolute path>"        # path to DevelopmentScript JSON
 230: source_root     = "<absolute path>"        # path to cloned/local source repo
 231: 
 232: [checkpoint]
 233: completed_stages = ["stage1", "stage2"]    # names of finished stages
 234: next_stage       = "<name or absent>"      # absent means all done
 235: last_updated_at  = "<RFC-3339>"
 236: status           = "started"               # started | in_progress | completed |
 237:                                            # interrupted | failed
 238: ```
 239: 
 240: ### `status` values
 241: 
 242: | Value | Meaning |
 243: |---|---|
 244: | `started` | Session created; no stages completed yet |
 245: | `in_progress` | At least one stage done; more remain |
 246: | `completed` | All stages finished |
 247: | `interrupted` | Process received Ctrl-C or SIGTERM mid-stage |
 248: | `failed` | Non-recoverable error; manual inspection needed |
 249: 
 250: Both `interrupted` and `in_progress` are resumable. `completed` is not. `failed` may require manual intervention — check the logs.
 251: 
 252: ---
 253: 
 254: ## Implementation notes
 255: 
 256: - `session.rs` owns all session state logic (load/save/complete_stage/mark_interrupted).
 257: - Saving is synchronous and uses `std::fs::write` — TOML is small (<1 KB) so there's no need for async I/O. The write happens after each stage completes, before the next stage begins.
 258: - The session file is written to the **destination** `.ghostwriter/` directory, not the source repo. This keeps the source repo clean.
 259: - `session.toml` is listed in the source repo's `.gitignore` recommendation, but lives in the destination, so it's naturally excluded from the source repo.

===== /docs/CONFIGURATION.md =====

   1: # ghostwriter — Configuration Reference
   2: 
   3: ghostwriter uses **two separate files** with different purposes:
   4: 
   5: | File | Location | Purpose | Managed by |
   6: |---|---|---|---|
   7: | `ghostwriter.toml` | Working directory (or `--config FILE`) | Behaviour settings — typing speed, typo rate, skip patterns, VSCode timing | You |
   8: | `.ghostwriter/session.toml` | `<dest>/.ghostwriter/session.toml` | Runtime/session state — repo URL, completed stages, checkpoint | ghostwriter |
   9: 
  10: Only `ghostwriter.toml` needs to be edited by hand. `session.toml` is written and updated automatically — see `docs/CHECKPOINT.md` for its full schema.
  11: 
  12: CLI flags override `ghostwriter.toml` where both exist. The priority order is:
  13: 
  14: ```
  15: compiled-in default  <  ghostwriter.toml  <  --config FILE  <  CLI flags
  16: ```
  17: 
  18: ---
  19: 
  20: ## `ghostwriter.toml` file structure
  21: 
  22: ```toml
  23: [typing]
  24: # ... typing simulation parameters
  25: 
  26: [typing.pause]
  27: # ... pause timing parameters
  28: 
  29: [repo]
  30: # ... repository ingestion parameters
  31: 
  32: [vscode]
  33: # ... VSCode interaction timing
  34: ```
  35: 
  36: ---
  37: 
  38: ## `[typing]`
  39: 
  40: Controls how keypresses are simulated. These are the most commonly tuned parameters.
  41: 
  42: ### `wpm`
  43: 
  44: **Type:** integer  
  45: **Default:** `60`  
  46: **CLI:** `--wpm N`  
  47: **Valid range:** 10–300  
  48: 
  49: Target typing speed in words per minute. One word = 5 characters. This sets the *median* keystroke delay — individual keystrokes vary around it due to `jitter`. At 60 WPM the median delay is ~200ms/char. At 120 WPM it is ~100ms/char.
  50: 
  51: Setting this above ~150 makes the typing look slightly less human (the right tail of the log-normal distribution compresses). 60–90 is the sweet spot for convincing recordings.
  52: 
  53: ```toml
  54: [typing]
  55: wpm = 75
  56: ```
  57: 
  58: ---
  59: 
  60: ### `jitter`
  61: 
  62: **Type:** float  
  63: **Default:** `0.30`  
  64: **Valid range:** 0.05–1.0  
  65: 
  66: The standard deviation of keystroke delay as a fraction of the mean. This is the `σ` parameter of the `LogNormal(μ, σ)` distribution used for per-character timing.
  67: 
  68: A value of `0.0` would produce perfectly uniform timing (robotic). `0.30` means 30% standard deviation — most keystrokes are near the target speed but occasional longer pauses occur naturally. Values above `0.6` produce erratic, visibly inconsistent typing.
  69: 
  70: ```toml
  71: [typing]
  72: jitter = 0.25   # slightly tighter than default
  73: ```
  74: 
  75: ---
  76: 
  77: ### `typo_rate`
  78: 
  79: **Type:** float  
  80: **Default:** `0.008`  
  81: **Valid range:** 0.0–0.05  
  82: 
  83: Probability of making a typo on any given character. `0.008` means roughly 1 typo per 125 characters — about one every 3–4 lines of code, which is realistic for moderate typing speed.
  84: 
  85: Setting to `0.0` disables typos entirely (useful for short demos or debugging). Setting above `0.02` produces visibly error-prone typing. The four typo kinds (adjacent substitution, extra char, accidental enter, transposition) are weighted internally — `typo_rate` controls only overall frequency.
  86: 
  87: ```toml
  88: [typing]
  89: typo_rate = 0.005   # fewer typos
  90: ```
  91: 
  92: ---
  93: 
  94: ## `[typing.pause]`
  95: 
  96: These control the extra pauses inserted at syntactically meaningful points. All values are millisecond ranges `[min, max]` — the actual pause is sampled uniformly from the range.
  97: 
  98: ### `after_newline`
  99: 
 100: **Type:** `[min_ms, max_ms]`  
 101: **Default:** `[200, 600]`  
 102: 
 103: Pause after pressing Enter. Simulates the brief moment a developer takes to orient before starting the next line.
 104: 
 105: ---
 106: 
 107: ### `after_open_block`
 108: 
 109: **Type:** `[min_ms, max_ms]`  
 110: **Default:** `[400, 1200]`  
 111: 
 112: Pause after `{` or `:` — entering a new block or match arm. Longer than `after_newline` because this often represents a decision point.
 113: 
 114: ---
 115: 
 116: ### `after_close_block`
 117: 
 118: **Type:** `[min_ms, max_ms]`  
 119: **Default:** `[250, 800]`  
 120: 
 121: Pause after `}` — finishing a block. Slightly shorter than `after_open_block`.
 122: 
 123: ---
 124: 
 125: ### `think_every_chars`
 126: 
 127: **Type:** `[min_chars, max_chars]`  
 128: **Default:** `[40, 80]`  
 129: 
 130: How often a spontaneous "thinking pause" is inserted. Every N characters (N sampled from this range), a thinking pause fires. At 60 WPM and default settings, this is roughly one pause every 8–16 seconds.
 131: 
 132: ---
 133: 
 134: ### `think_duration`
 135: 
 136: **Type:** `[min_ms, max_ms]`  
 137: **Default:** `[800, 2500]`  
 138: 
 139: How long a thinking pause lasts. The default range (0.8–2.5 seconds) represents the brief pause a developer takes to plan the next few lines, look something up, or re-read what they just wrote. Longer values (e.g. `[2000, 8000]`) simulate a slower, more deliberate developer.
 140: 
 141: ```toml
 142: [typing.pause]
 143: after_newline       = [150, 400]
 144: after_open_block    = [300, 900]
 145: after_close_block   = [200, 600]
 146: think_every_chars   = [60, 120]   # less frequent thinking pauses
 147: think_duration      = [500, 1800]
 148: ```
 149: 
 150: ---
 151: 
 152: ## `[repo]`
 153: 
 154: Controls which files are included in playback.
 155: 
 156: ### `skip`
 157: 
 158: **Type:** array of glob strings  
 159: **Default:**
 160: 
 161: ```toml
 162: [repo]
 163: skip = [
 164:     ".git/**",
 165:     "node_modules/**",
 166:     "target/**",
 167:     "dist/**",
 168:     "*.lock",
 169:     "*.sum",
 170: ]
 171: ```
 172: 
 173: Glob patterns for files to exclude from analysis and playback. Patterns are matched against the relative path from the repo root. Adding patterns with `--skip` on the CLI appends to this list rather than replacing it.
 174: 
 175: Common additions:
 176: 
 177: ```toml
 178: [repo]
 179: skip = [
 180:     ".git/**",
 181:     "target/**",
 182:     "*.lock",
 183:     "*.sum",
 184:     "docs/**",          # skip documentation
 185:     "benches/**",       # skip benchmarks
 186:     "examples/**",      # skip examples
 187: ]
 188: ```
 189: 
 190: ---
 191: 
 192: ### `use_git_order`
 193: 
 194: **Type:** boolean  
 195: **Default:** `false`  
 196: 
 197: When `true`, ghostwriter attempts to order files according to the order they first appeared in the git history, rather than using topological sort by dependency. This can produce a more historically accurate playback for repos with a clear commit-by-commit progression.
 198: 
 199: Currently not fully implemented — setting this to `true` falls back to dependency order. See PROJECT_STATUS.md.
 200: 
 201: ```toml
 202: [repo]
 203: use_git_order = false
 204: ```
 205: 
 206: ---
 207: 
 208: ## `[vscode]`
 209: 
 210: Timing delays for VSCode interactions. These rarely need tuning unless VSCode is running slowly on the target machine (old hardware, heavy extensions, large workspace).
 211: 
 212: ### `launch_delay_ms`
 213: 
 214: **Type:** integer (milliseconds)  
 215: **Default:** `2500`  
 216: **Valid range:** 500–10000  
 217: 
 218: How long to wait after launching VSCode before sending any keystrokes. VSCode needs time to fully load the workspace, load extensions, and display the Explorer sidebar. On a slow machine or a large repo, increase this.
 219: 
 220: The bootstrap preflight also performs active polling (checks for the activity bar becoming visible), so this is a *minimum* floor rather than the only safeguard.
 221: 
 222: ```toml
 223: [vscode]
 224: launch_delay_ms = 3500   # slow machine or large repo
 225: ```
 226: 
 227: ---
 228: 
 229: ### `file_open_delay_ms`
 230: 
 231: **Type:** integer (milliseconds)  
 232: **Default:** `400`  
 233: 
 234: How long to wait after clicking a file in the Explorer sidebar before the editor is considered ready. This covers the time for the file to load, syntax highlighting to render, and any LSP activity to settle.
 235: 
 236: Increase this if you're recording a repo with heavy language server activity (e.g. a large workspace where rust-analyzer takes time to index). Decrease it if the recording feels slow — `200ms` is usually sufficient on fast hardware.
 237: 
 238: ```toml
 239: [vscode]
 240: file_open_delay_ms = 300   # fast machine
 241: ```
 242: 
 243: ---
 244: 
 245: ## Navigator constants (not yet configurable)
 246: 
 247: These are hardcoded in the current version. They will become configurable in a future release. If you need to change them, edit the source directly.
 248: 
 249: | Constant | Location | Default | Description |
 250: |---|---|---|---|
 251: | `match_threshold` | `navigator/dictionary.rs` | `0.82` | NCC score required to accept a template match. Lower = more matches but more false positives. Higher = fewer fallbacks but more CLI fallbacks on ambiguous rows. |
 252: | `MIN_TEMPLATE_PCT` | `navigator/bootstrap.rs` | `80%` | Minimum percentage of files that must be successfully captured during bootstrap. |
 253: | `MAX_VERIFY_FAILURES` | `navigator/bootstrap.rs` | `2` | Maximum number of verification failures tolerated in the Step 9 sample. |
 254: | `VSCODE_LAUNCH_TIMEOUT_MS` | `navigator/bootstrap.rs` | `6000ms` | How long bootstrap waits for VSCode's activity bar to become visible. |
 255: | `ACTIVITY_BAR_CENTRE_X` | `navigator/bootstrap.rs` | `24px` | Assumed logical x-coordinate of VSCode activity bar centre. Calibrated for default VSCode layout. |
 256: 
 257: ---
 258: 
 259: ## Full example `ghostwriter.toml`
 260: 
 261: ```toml
 262: [typing]
 263: wpm        = 75
 264: jitter     = 0.28
 265: typo_rate  = 0.006
 266: 
 267: [typing.pause]
 268: after_newline     = [150, 500]
 269: after_open_block  = [350, 1100]
 270: after_close_block = [200, 700]
 271: think_every_chars = [50, 100]
 272: think_duration    = [700, 2200]
 273: 
 274: [repo]
 275: skip = [
 276:     ".git/**",
 277:     "target/**",
 278:     "*.lock",
 279:     "*.sum",
 280:     "benches/**",
 281: ]
 282: use_git_order = false
 283: 
 284: [vscode]
 285: launch_delay_ms   = 2500
 286: file_open_delay_ms = 350
 287: ```
 288: 
 289: ---
 290: 
 291: ## Priority order
 292: 
 293: When the same setting is specified in multiple places, the last one wins:
 294: 
 295: ```
 296: compiled-in default  <  ghostwriter.toml  <  --config FILE  <  CLI flags
 297: ```
 298: 
 299: For example, `--wpm 120` always overrides `wpm = 60` in `ghostwriter.toml`.

===== /docs/DATA_FLOW.md =====

   1: # ghostwriter — Data Flow
   2: 
   3: This document traces every significant piece of data from the moment ghostwriter starts to the moment the last character is typed. Each section shows what is produced, what consumes it, and what format it takes.
   4: 
   5: ---
   6: 
   7: ## 1. Top-Level Pipeline
   8: 
   9: ```mermaid
  10: flowchart TD
  11:     CLI["CLI Args\n--repo URL\n--dest PATH\n--wpm N\n--seed N"]
  12:     TOML["ghostwriter.toml\n(optional)"]
  13:     CLI --> Config
  14:     TOML --> Config
  15: 
  16:     Config["Config\n{ typing, repo, vscode }"]
  17: 
  18:     Config --> Fetch
  19: 
  20:     Fetch["repo::fetch()\ngit2 shallow clone\nor resolve local path"]
  21:     Fetch -->|"source_root: PathBuf"| Orch
  22: 
  23:     Orch["orchestrator::orchestrate()\nanalyze → plan → AI tasks → assemble"]
  24:     Orch -->|"DevelopmentScript (JSON-serialisable)"| Cache{{"Cache?\n.ghostwriter/script.json"}}
  25:     Cache -->|"hit: load JSON"| Script
  26:     Cache -->|"miss: generate + save"| Script
  27: 
  28:     Script["DevelopmentScript\n{ stages: Vec<Stage> }"]
  29:     Script --> DryRun{{"--dry-run?"}}
  30:     DryRun -->|yes| Print["print stage plan\n→ exit"]
  31:     DryRun -->|no| Boot
  32: 
  33:     Boot["navigator::bootstrap()\n9-step sequence on source_root"]
  34:     Boot -->|"BootstrapResult\n{ dictionary, layout }"| Open
  35: 
  36:     Open["typer::vscode::open(dest)\nopen empty destination in VSCode"]
  37:     Open --> Engine
  38: 
  39:     Engine["typer::engine::run()\nexecute script actions"]
  40:     Engine --> Done(["✓ Done"])
  41: ```
  42: 
  43: ---
  44: 
  45: ## 2. Orchestrator Data Flow
  46: 
  47: ```mermaid
  48: flowchart TD
  49:     SrcRoot["source_root: PathBuf"] --> Walk
  50: 
  51:     Walk["walkdir::WalkDir\nall .rs files"] --> Analyze
  52: 
  53:     Analyze["analyzer::analyze()\nper-file:"]
  54:     Analyze -->|"rel_path, content\ncategory, complexity\nlocal_deps"| FileInfo["Vec&lt;FileInfo&gt;"]
  55: 
  56:     FileInfo --> Planner
  57: 
  58:     subgraph Planner["stage_planner::plan()"]
  59:         Topo["toposort()\nKahn's algorithm\nover dep graph"]
  60:         Depths["topo_depths()\nDAG depth per file"]
  61:         Bucket["bucket into stages:\n1. skeleton (Config, EntryPoint)\n2. types (Types)\n3. impl_0, impl_1 … (Impl by depth)\n4. tests (Tests)\n5. misc (Misc)"]
  62:         Topo --> Depths
  63:         Depths --> Bucket
  64:     end
  65: 
  66:     Planner -->|"StagePlan\n{ stages: Vec<PlannedStage> }"| Tasks
  67: 
  68:     subgraph Tasks["orchestrator::build_tasks()"]
  69:         PerSlot["for each FileSlot:"]
  70:         GenStub["AiTask::GenerateStub\n{ final_content }"]
  71:         GenInter["AiTask::GenerateIntermediates\n{ stub, final, count }"]
  72:         DescTrans["AiTask::DescribeTransition\n{ before_summary, after_summary }"]
  73:         PerSlot --> GenStub
  74:         PerSlot --> GenInter
  75:         PerSlot --> DescTrans
  76:     end
  77: 
  78:     Tasks -->|"Vec&lt;AiTask&gt;"| LLM
  79: 
  80:     LLM["Anthropic API\nclaude-* model"] -->|"raw JSON\nper-task"| Parse
  81: 
  82:     Parse["AiResult::parse(task, json)"]
  83:     Parse -->|"AiResult::{Stub,\nIntermediates, Description}"| Assemble
  84: 
  85:     Assemble["assembler::assemble()"]
  86:     Assemble -->|"DevelopmentScript"| SerJson["serialize to JSON\n.ghostwriter/script.json"]
  87: ```
  88: 
  89: ---
  90: 
  91: ## 3. File Category Heuristics
  92: 
  93: The analyzer assigns each file a category using these rules in priority order:
  94: 
  95: | Category | Heuristic |
  96: |---|---|
  97: | `Config` | Filename matches `Cargo.toml`, `build.rs`, `*.toml`, `*.json`, `*.yaml` |
  98: | `EntryPoint` | Filename is `main.rs` or `lib.rs` |
  99: | `Tests` | Path contains `/tests/` or filename ends with `_test.rs` or `tests.rs`, OR file has `#[cfg(test)]` |
 100: | `Types` | Filename contains `types`, `model`, `schema`, `error`, `errors` |
 101: | `Impl` | Contains `impl ` keyword without being a test file |
 102: | `Misc` | Everything else |
 103: 
 104: Complexity is assigned by line count:
 105: 
 106: | Complexity | Line count | Intermediate versions |
 107: |---|---|---|
 108: | Trivial | < 30 | 0 |
 109: | Small | 30–100 | 1 |
 110: | Medium | 100–300 | 2 |
 111: | Large | > 300 | 3 |
 112: 
 113: ---
 114: 
 115: ## 4. Bootstrap Data Flow
 116: 
 117: ```mermaid
 118: flowchart TD
 119:     SrcRoot2["source_root: PathBuf"] --> VSCode2["open VSCode\nat source_root"]
 120: 
 121:     VSCode2 --> SS1["capture_screen()\nDynamicImage (physical px)"]
 122:     SS1 --> SD1["scale_down(img, scale_factor)\nDynamicImage (logical px)"]
 123:     SD1 --> Detect["probe_sidebar()\ncolor scan → Rect"]
 124: 
 125:     Detect -->|"sidebar_rect: Rect\n{ x=48, y=0, w=?, h=display_h }"| DictInit
 126: 
 127:     DictInit["VisualDictionary::new(dest, sidebar_rect)"]
 128: 
 129:     DictInit --> ForEach
 130: 
 131:     subgraph ForEach["for each rel_path in file_paths"]
 132:         OpenFile["vscode::open_file(abs_path)\nsleep 380ms"]
 133:         SS2["capture_screen()\nDynamicImage"]
 134:         ScaleDown2["scale_down(img, scale)\nDynamicImage (logical)"]
 135:         Crop["img.crop_imm(sidebar_rect)\nDynamicImage (sidebar only)"]
 136:         FindRow["find_selected_row(sidebar_img)\n→ Option&lt;u32&gt; (Y pixel)"]
 137:         CropRow["crop_row(img, y, row_height)\nDynamicImage (template strip)"]
 138:         Insert["dict.insert(rel_path, template, Some(screen_y))"]
 139:         RecordY["y_samples.push(screen_y)"]
 140: 
 141:         OpenFile --> SS2 --> ScaleDown2 --> Crop --> FindRow --> CropRow --> Insert --> RecordY
 142:     end
 143: 
 144:     RecordY --> RowHeight["measure_row_height(y_samples)\n→ u32 (median gap)"]
 145:     RowHeight --> Verify
 146: 
 147:     Verify["verify_sample()\n5 random files\nNCC check"] --> Save
 148: 
 149:     Save["dict.save()\n→ .ghostwriter/dictionary.json\n→ .ghostwriter/templates/*.png"]
 150:     Save --> Result["BootstrapResult\n{ dictionary, layout: VscodeLayout }"]
 151: ```
 152: 
 153: ---
 154: 
 155: ## 5. Template Capture in Detail
 156: 
 157: ```mermaid
 158: flowchart LR
 159:     subgraph Physical["Physical pixel space (raw screenshot)"]
 160:         PhysImg["DynamicImage\n2560×1600 px (Retina example)"]
 161:     end
 162: 
 163:     subgraph Logical["Logical pixel space (after ÷ scale_factor)"]
 164:         LogImg["DynamicImage\n1280×800 px"]
 165:         SidebarCrop["Sidebar crop\nRect { x:48, y:0, w:220, h:800 }"]
 166:         RowCrop["Row crop\n220×22 px\n← the template"]
 167:     end
 168: 
 169:     PhysImg -->|"÷ 2.0 (Retina)"| LogImg
 170:     LogImg -->|"crop_imm(48,0,220,800)"| SidebarCrop
 171:     SidebarCrop -->|"find selected row → y=145\ncrop_row(145, 22px)"| RowCrop
 172: 
 173:     RowCrop -->|"convert to Luma8\nfor theme robustness"| Template["Template PNG\n220×22 px (greyscale)\nsaved to .ghostwriter/templates/"]
 174: ```
 175: 
 176: ---
 177: 
 178: ## 6. Playback Data Flow
 179: 
 180: ```mermaid
 181: flowchart TD
 182:     Script2["DevelopmentScript\n{ stages }"] --> StageLoop
 183: 
 184:     subgraph StageLoop["engine: for each stage"]
 185:         ActionLoop["for each action"]
 186: 
 187:         subgraph CreateFileFlow["Action::CreateFile"]
 188:             CF1["navigator::open(path)\n→ click file in sidebar"]
 189:             CF2["type stub character by character\nhuman::delay_for() per char\nhuman::maybe_typo() per char"]
 190:             CF3["for each intermediate:\n  navigator::open(path)\n  apply Edit { find, replace }"]
 191:             CF4["apply final content edits"]
 192:             CF5["navigator::capture_new_file(path)\n→ capture template for new file"]
 193:             CF1 --> CF2 --> CF3 --> CF4 --> CF5
 194:         end
 195: 
 196:         subgraph EditFileFlow["Action::EditFile"]
 197:             EF1["navigator::open(path)"]
 198:             EF2["for each Edit { find, replace }:\n  Ctrl+H (find+replace)\n  type find string\n  type replace string\n  Confirm"]
 199:             EF1 --> EF2
 200:         end
 201: 
 202:         subgraph CursorFlow["Action::MoveCursor"]
 203:             MC1["navigator::open(path)"]
 204:             MC2["editor::goto_line(line)"]
 205:             MC1 --> MC2
 206:         end
 207: 
 208:         subgraph CommandFlow["Action::RunCommand"]
 209:             RC1["editor::open_terminal()"]
 210:             RC2["type command\npress Enter"]
 211:             RC1 --> RC2
 212:         end
 213: 
 214:         subgraph PauseFlow["Action::ThinkPause"]
 215:             TP1["tokio::time::sleep(duration)"]
 216:         end
 217: 
 218:         ActionLoop --> CreateFileFlow
 219:         ActionLoop --> EditFileFlow
 220:         ActionLoop --> CursorFlow
 221:         ActionLoop --> CommandFlow
 222:         ActionLoop --> PauseFlow
 223:     end
 224: ```
 225: 
 226: ---
 227: 
 228: ## 7. Human Simulator Data Flow
 229: 
 230: ```mermaid
 231: flowchart LR
 232:     Char["next char\nfrom content string"] --> MaybeTypo
 233: 
 234:     MaybeTypo["human::maybe_typo(char)\nroll: rand() < typo_rate?"]
 235: 
 236:     MaybeTypo -->|"no typo"| NormalDelay
 237:     MaybeTypo -->|"AdjacentSub"| TypoAdj
 238:     MaybeTypo -->|"ExtraChar"| TypoExtra
 239:     MaybeTypo -->|"AccidentalEnter"| TypoEnter
 240:     MaybeTypo -->|"Transposition"| TypoTrans
 241: 
 242:     NormalDelay["delay_for(char)\nLogNormal(μ,σ)\nbased on WPM config"] --> TypeChar["enigo.text(char)"]
 243: 
 244:     TypoAdj["type wrong_char\nsleep notice_after\nBackspace\ntype correct_char"] --> TypeChar
 245:     TypoExtra["type correct_char + extra\nsleep notice_after\nBackspace"] --> TypeChar
 246:     TypoEnter["press Enter\nsleep notice_after\nBackspace + re-indent"] --> TypeChar
 247:     TypoTrans["type second_char first\ntype first_char second\nsleep notice_after\n2× Backspace + retype both"] --> TypeChar
 248: 
 249:     TypeChar --> ThinkCheck["think_counter++\nif counter > next_threshold:\n  sleep(think_duration)\n  reset counter"] --> NextChar["next char"]
 250: ```
 251: 
 252: ---
 253: 
 254: ## 8. Visual Navigation Data Flow (during playback)
 255: 
 256: ```mermaid
 257: flowchart TD
 258:     NavOpen["navigator::open(rel_path)"] --> EnsureParents
 259: 
 260:     EnsureParents["sidebar::ensure_parents_expanded()\nexpand parent dirs if collapsed"] --> TryClick
 261: 
 262:     TryClick["try_click(rel_path)  [up to 3 times]"]
 263: 
 264:     TryClick --> SS3["capture_screen()\nDynamicImage"]
 265:     SS3 --> SD3["scale_down(÷ scale_factor)"]
 266:     SD3 --> Crop3["crop to sidebar_rect"]
 267: 
 268:     Crop3 --> HintCheck{{"last_known_y?"}}
 269:     HintCheck -->|"Some(y)"| FindNear["template::find_near\n(sidebar, template, y, radius=200)"]
 270:     HintCheck -->|"None"| FindFull["template::find\n(sidebar, template)\nfull scan"]
 271: 
 272:     FindNear --> Score
 273:     FindFull --> Score
 274: 
 275:     Score["MatchResult { x, y, score }"]
 276:     Score --> Threshold{{"score ≥ threshold?"}}
 277: 
 278:     Threshold -->|"yes"| Click["mouse::click\n(screen_x, screen_y)\nBézier curve movement"]
 279:     Threshold -->|"no, attempt < 3"| Scroll["scroll_sidebar(3 ticks)\nsleep 200ms"] --> TryClick
 280:     Threshold -->|"no, attempt = 3"| Fallback["CLI fallback:\nvscode::open_file(abs_path)"]
 281: 
 282:     Click --> UpdatePos["dict.update_position\n(rel_path, new_y, score)"]
 283: ```
 284: 
 285: ---
 286: 
 287: ## 9. Coordinate Space Summary
 288: 
 289: All coordinate handling in ghostwriter must respect two spaces:
 290: 
 291: | Space | Unit | Source | Used for |
 292: |---|---|---|---|
 293: | Physical pixels | Raw screen pixels | `capture_screen()` | Storing template PNGs |
 294: | Logical pixels | Physical ÷ scale_factor | After `scale_down()` | All mouse coordinates and template matching |
 295: 
 296: The `scale_factor` is stored in `VscodeLayout` and applied consistently. On a standard 1:1 display, `scale_factor = 1.0` and the two spaces are identical.
 297: 
 298: ```mermaid
 299: flowchart LR
 300:     SS["📸 Screenshot\n(physical pixels)\ne.g. 2560×1600"]
 301:     SD["÷ scale_factor\ne.g. ÷ 2.0 on Retina"]
 302:     LP["Logical pixels\ne.g. 1280×800"]
 303:     TM["Template matching\nNCC on logical image"]
 304:     MC["🖱️ Mouse coordinates\nenigo.move_mouse(x, y)"]
 305: 
 306:     SS -->|"raw capture"| SD
 307:     SD --> LP
 308:     LP --> TM
 309:     LP --> MC
 310:     TM -->|"match result x,y"| MC
 311: ```

===== /docs/DESIGN.md =====

   1: # ghostwriter — Design Document
   2: 
   3: **Document status:** Living document  
   4: **Last revised:** 2026-05  
   5: 
   6: ---
   7: 
   8: ## 1. Problem Statement
   9: 
  10: Content creators, educators, and developers want to record themselves "writing" a code project from scratch. The honest approach — actually typing the project live — is error-prone, slow, and hard to reproduce. The naive approach — screen-recording a `cp -r` — is obviously fake.
  11: 
  12: ghostwriter solves this by replaying a synthetic but behaviorally realistic coding session. The source of truth is the finished repo; ghostwriter reverse-engineers how a developer might have written it, then performs that story back in real time inside VSCode.
  13: 
  14: ---
  15: 
  16: ## 2. Goals
  17: 
  18: | Goal | Priority |
  19: |---|---|
  20: | The typing looks human | Must have |
  21: | File ordering reflects real developer intent | Must have |
  22: | Mouse navigates to files visually (not via CLI) | Must have |
  23: | Any Rust repo works without configuration | Must have |
  24: | Reproducible given same seed | Should have |
  25: | Sub-3-minute setup for ≤50-file repos | Should have |
  26: | Works on macOS, Linux, Windows | Should have |
  27: | Configurable speed and typo rate | Nice to have |
  28: 
  29: ---
  30: 
  31: ## 3. Non-Goals
  32: 
  33: - **Not a code generator** — ghostwriter types existing code verbatim; it never invents.
  34: - **Not a recorder** — ghostwriter drives VSCode live; it does not post-process video.
  35: - **Not language-agnostic** — Rust only in this version.
  36: - **Not streaming-capable** — there is no live output API.
  37: 
  38: ---
  39: 
  40: ## 4. Key Design Decisions
  41: 
  42: ### Decision 1: Run Bootstrap on the Complete Source Repo
  43: 
  44: **Problem:** To navigate by clicking files in VSCode's Explorer, we need visual templates of those files — i.e. screenshots of each file being selected. But we can't take those screenshots before the files exist.
  45: 
  46: **Decision:** Bootstrap runs against the already-cloned source repo (not the empty destination). VSCode opens the complete repo so every file template can be captured. Templates are keyed by *relative* path so they remain valid when playback writes into the destination.
  47: 
  48: **Alternative considered:** Bootstrap against an early partial build. Rejected — the number of visible files changes as files are created, shifting row positions for all subsequent matches.
  49: 
  50: ---
  51: 
  52: ### Decision 2: AI Does As Little As Possible
  53: 
  54: **Problem:** LLM calls are slow, expensive, and non-deterministic. Early designs had the LLM producing the full DevelopmentScript.
  55: 
  56: **Decision:** Rust handles all structural work. The LLM receives only narrow, well-scoped tasks:
  57: 
  58: | Task | What Rust provides | What LLM produces |
  59: |---|---|---|
  60: | GenerateStub | Final file content | Stubbed version (signatures only) |
  61: | GenerateIntermediates | Stub + final content, count | N intermediate whole-file versions |
  62: | DescribeTransition | Before+after diff summary | One sentence (8–15 words) |
  63: 
  64: This means the same three prompts work for *any* Rust project without modification.
  65: 
  66: **Alternative considered:** LLM decides file order, stage breakdown, and all content. Rejected — too slow, too expensive, highly inconsistent results.
  67: 
  68: ---
  69: 
  70: ### Decision 3: Three Fixed Prompt Templates
  71: 
  72: **Problem:** If prompts are dynamically constructed per-project, they must be re-validated for every repo. Maintenance burden scales with the number of projects.
  73: 
  74: **Decision:** Three prompt templates are fixed at build time. Only the injected *data* changes (file content, diff summaries, counts). The templates are treated as contracts: they specify output format precisely (JSON, specific keys) and enumerate exactly what the LLM must and must not do.
  75: 
  76: **Trade-off:** Less flexibility in how the AI interprets intent, but much more reliable output that Rust code can parse deterministically.
  77: 
  78: ---
  79: 
  80: ### Decision 4: NCC Template Matching for File Navigation
  81: 
  82: **Problem:** To click a file in VSCode's Explorer sidebar, we need to know its Y-coordinate. But row positions shift as folders expand/collapse and new files are created.
  83: 
  84: **Decision:** During bootstrap, we open each file and capture a screenshot of the sidebar row that becomes selected/highlighted. We store this as a template image. During playback, we run Normalised Cross-Correlation (NCC) on the current sidebar screenshot to find the best match. NCC is used because:
  85: - It is insensitive to absolute brightness (works for dark and light themes)
  86: - It is fast on small images (sidebar strip, ~280px wide)
  87: - It localises accurately to ±1 pixel
  88: 
  89: **Alternative considered:** OCR the filename text in the sidebar. Rejected — filename text is rendered at sub-pixel weights, varies with font smoothing settings, and requires a heavy dependency.
  90: 
  91: ---
  92: 
  93: ### Decision 5: Bézier Curve Mouse Movement
  94: 
  95: **Problem:** A mouse that teleports to exact pixel coordinates looks robotic.
  96: 
  97: **Decision:** All mouse movement uses a quadratic Bézier curve with a randomised control point offset. Each 8ms step advances along the curve. Pre- and post-click delays are sampled from a uniform random range.
  98: 
  99: **Trade-off:** Slightly slower than direct movement, but the smoothness cost is negligible at human scale (a 300ms move).
 100: 
 101: ---
 102: 
 103: ### Decision 6: Log-Normal Typing Delays
 104: 
 105: **Problem:** Uniform random delays don't match real typing data. Real typing has a right-skewed distribution — most keystrokes are fast, but occasional long pauses exist (thinking, error recovery).
 106: 
 107: **Decision:** Per-character delay is sampled from a `LogNormal(μ, σ)` distribution. The parameters are derived from the configured WPM target. The distribution is always positive (no zero-length delays) and naturally produces the occasional longer pause without special-casing.
 108: 
 109: **Alternative considered:** Gaussian distribution. Rejected — can produce negative values, requires clamping, and doesn't match empirical keystroke timing data.
 110: 
 111: ---
 112: 
 113: ### Decision 7: Typed Error Variants for Bootstrap
 114: 
 115: **Problem:** Bootstrap failure is the most common user-facing issue. `anyhow::bail!("something failed")` gives no actionable guidance.
 116: 
 117: **Decision:** `BootstrapError` is a `thiserror`-derived enum with 14 distinct variants. Every variant encodes:
 118: 1. The exact step that failed (with step number)
 119: 2. The most likely causes (bullet list)
 120: 3. Specific remediation steps per platform
 121: 4. The path to a saved diagnostic screenshot
 122: 
 123: This turns a confusing failure into a self-service fix in most cases.
 124: 
 125: ---
 126: 
 127: ## 5. System Overview
 128: 
 129: ![System Overview](diagrams/system-overview.svg)
 130: 
 131: ---
 132: 
 133: ## 6. Component Responsibilities
 134: 
 135: ### `repo`
 136: Clones or resolves the source repository to a local path. Returns the root `PathBuf`. Supports HTTPS URLs and local paths.
 137: 
 138: ### `orchestrator`
 139: Pure analysis + script generation. No I/O after the clone step. Produces a `DevelopmentScript` that can be serialised to JSON and replayed later. Contains:
 140: - `analyzer` — file categorisation, complexity, dependency graph
 141: - `stage_planner` — topological sort, stage bucketing
 142: - `tasks` / `prompts` — LLM task types and fixed templates
 143: - `assembler` — combines AI results into the final script
 144: 
 145: ### `navigator`
 146: Owns the visual coordination layer. Does two jobs:
 147: 1. **Bootstrap** — one-time setup that captures templates for every file
 148: 2. **Navigation** — during playback, finds and clicks files in the Explorer
 149: 
 150: ### `typer`
 151: Executes the script. Does three jobs:
 152: 1. **`engine`** — walks stages and actions, dispatches to human/editor/navigator
 153: 2. **`human`** — per-character timing and typo simulation
 154: 3. **`editor`** — higher-level VSCode commands (goto line, select region)
 155: 
 156: ---
 157: 
 158: ## 7. Data Model Summary
 159: 
 160: The two central data structures that flow between all subsystems:
 161: 
 162: ```mermaid
 163: graph LR
 164:     subgraph Script["DevelopmentScript (serialisable JSON)"]
 165:         DS[DevelopmentScript]
 166:         ST[Stage\nname, description]
 167:         AC[Action]
 168:         CF["CreateFile\npath, stub, intermediates, final"]
 169:         EF["EditFile\npath, edits: Vec&lt;Edit&gt;"]
 170:         MC["MoveCursor\npath, line"]
 171:         RC["RunCommand\ncommand"]
 172:         TP["ThinkPause\nduration_ms"]
 173:         DS -->|"Vec&lt;Stage&gt;"| ST
 174:         ST -->|"Vec&lt;Action&gt;"| AC
 175:         AC --- CF & EF & MC & RC & TP
 176:     end
 177: 
 178:     subgraph Dict["VisualDictionary (persisted to .ghostwriter/)"]
 179:         VD[VisualDictionary\nsidebar_region\nmatch_threshold\nrow_height_px]
 180:         DE[DictEntry]
 181:         TM["template: DynamicImage\n(PNG sidebar row strip)"]
 182:         EM["EntryMeta\nlast_known_y\nconfidence\nparent_dirs"]
 183:         VD -->|"HashMap&lt;PathBuf, DictEntry&gt;"| DE
 184:         DE --- TM & EM
 185:     end
 186: ```
 187: 
 188: ---
 189: 
 190: ## 8. Error Handling Strategy
 191: 
 192: | Layer | Strategy |
 193: |---|---|
 194: | `repo` | `anyhow::Result` — I/O errors with context |
 195: | `orchestrator` | `anyhow::Result` — includes LLM response parsing errors |
 196: | `navigator::bootstrap` | `BootstrapError` enum — typed, user-facing, with diagnostics |
 197: | `navigator::open` | `anyhow::Result` — falls back to CLI on visual nav failure |
 198: | `typer::engine` | `anyhow::Result` — propagates to main |
 199: | `main` | Prints error chain and exits with non-zero code |
 200: 
 201: The `print_fatal()` method on `BootstrapError` renders a bordered error box to stderr before the process exits, ensuring the failure is unmissable even in scrolling terminal output.
 202: 
 203: ---
 204: 
 205: ## 9. Configuration Philosophy
 206: 
 207: All timing, threshold, and behaviour parameters are configurable via `ghostwriter.toml`. Defaults are tuned to be realistic out of the box (60 WPM, 0.008 typo rate, 0.82 NCC threshold). No configuration file is required for a first run.

===== /docs/GLOSSARY.md =====

   1: # ghostwriter — Glossary
   2: 
   3: Precise definitions for terms used throughout the codebase, documentation, and comments. When a term appears in code or docs, it has exactly the meaning defined here — no synonyms, no variations.
   4: 
   5: ---
   6: 
   7: ## Coordinate Spaces
   8: 
   9: ### Physical pixels
  10: Raw pixel coordinates as returned by `capture_screen()`. On a Retina/HiDPI display, these are 2× (or more) the logical pixel count. **Never passed directly to `enigo`.** All physical pixel values come from the `screenshots` crate.
  11: 
  12: ### Logical pixels
  13: Display coordinates as understood by the window system and `enigo`. On a standard 1:1 display, logical = physical. On a Retina MacBook, logical = physical ÷ 2. All mouse movement and click coordinates must be in logical pixels. Convert using `scale_down()` or explicit division by `scale_factor`.
  14: 
  15: ### `scale_factor`
  16: The ratio `physical_pixels / logical_pixels`. Stored in `VscodeLayout`. On a standard display `scale_factor = 1.0`. On a 2× Retina display `scale_factor = 2.0`. The invariant: `enigo_coordinate = screenshot_coordinate / scale_factor`.
  17: 
  18: ### `Rect`
  19: A rectangle defined by `{ x, y, width, height }` in **physical pixels** as declared in `screen.rs`. When a `Rect` is used for mouse targeting, its coordinates must be divided by `scale_factor` first.
  20: 
  21: ---
  22: 
  23: ## Visual Navigation
  24: 
  25: ### Template
  26: A small greyscale PNG image capturing what a specific file's sidebar row looks like when it is selected/highlighted in VSCode's Explorer. Cropped from a screenshot during bootstrap. Stored as `DynamicImage` in memory and persisted to `.ghostwriter/templates/<sanitised_path>.png`. Templates are in **physical pixel** dimensions.
  27: 
  28: ### Template matching / NCC
  29: The process of finding a template image inside a larger sidebar screenshot using Normalised Cross-Correlation. Produces a `MatchResult { x, y, score }`. NCC is insensitive to absolute brightness, making it work across dark and light VSCode themes.
  30: 
  31: ### `MatchResult`
  32: The output of `template::find()` or `template::find_near()`. Contains the top-left corner `(x, y)` of the best match within the haystack, and a `score` in [0, 1]. A score ≥ `match_threshold` (default 0.82) is accepted as a valid match.
  33: 
  34: ### `match_threshold`
  35: The minimum NCC score required to accept a template match as valid. Stored in `VisualDictionary`. Default: `0.82`. Below this score, the navigator either retries (by scrolling and re-scanning) or falls back to the CLI.
  36: 
  37: ### VisualDictionary
  38: The in-memory (and persisted) collection of all templates, keyed by **relative path**. Also stores `last_known_y` (the last confirmed Y position of each file's row) and `sidebar_region` (the bounding `Rect` of the Explorer sidebar). Serialised to `.ghostwriter/dictionary.json`.
  39: 
  40: ### `last_known_y`
  41: The Y coordinate (in logical pixels) at which a file's sidebar row was last successfully matched. Used as the centre of the search window in `find_near()` to speed up subsequent lookups. Updated after every successful click.
  42: 
  43: ### `find_near()`
  44: An NCC search restricted to a vertical band `[hint_y - radius, hint_y + radius]` around a known previous position. Faster than a full scan. Falls back to `find()` if `last_known_y` is `None`.
  45: 
  46: ### Sidebar region
  47: The `Rect` (in physical pixels) that bounds VSCode's Explorer sidebar panel. Detected during bootstrap Step 6. Used to crop screenshots before running NCC — avoids scanning the entire display.
  48: 
  49: ### Row height
  50: The vertical pixel height of a single file row in the Explorer sidebar. Measured during bootstrap by taking the median Y-gap between consecutive file row positions. Stored in `VscodeLayout.row_height_px`. Typically 22px at standard zoom.
  51: 
  52: ---
  53: 
  54: ## Bootstrap
  55: 
  56: ### Bootstrap
  57: The one-time setup sequence that runs on the **source repo** before any playback begins. Produces a `BootstrapResult`. Opens VSCode, captures templates for every file in the project, and saves them to `.ghostwriter/`. See `docs/BOOTSTRAP.md` for the 9-step sequence.
  58: 
  59: ### `BootstrapResult`
  60: The output of a successful bootstrap: `{ dictionary: VisualDictionary, layout: VscodeLayout }`. Passed into the engine to enable visual navigation during playback.
  61: 
  62: ### `VscodeLayout`
  63: Captures the display geometry detected during bootstrap: `scale_factor`, `display_width`, `display_height`, `row_height_px`. Stable for the duration of a playback session (assumes no display configuration changes after bootstrap).
  64: 
  65: ### `BootstrapError`
  66: A typed enum (14 variants) representing every distinct failure mode of the bootstrap sequence. Each variant encodes the failed step, likely causes, and remediation instructions. Rendered to stderr via `print_fatal()` before the process exits.
  67: 
  68: ### Diagnostic screenshot
  69: A screenshot saved to `.ghostwriter/diagnostics/` at the point where a bootstrap step fails. Provides visual evidence of the screen state at failure time. Named after the step that failed (e.g. `step3_fullscreen_failed.png`). Saved on a best-effort basis — failure to save a diagnostic never masks the original error.
  70: 
  71: ### `preflight_check()`
  72: Step 1 of bootstrap. Verifies three prerequisites before any I/O: the `code` CLI is on PATH, screen capture returns a non-black frame (permission not denied), and input injection succeeds (Accessibility permission granted).
  73: 
  74: ---
  75: 
  76: ## Orchestration
  77: 
  78: ### DevelopmentScript
  79: The central data structure produced by the orchestrator and consumed by the engine. A list of `Stage`s, each containing a list of `Action`s. JSON-serialisable; can be cached to `.ghostwriter/script.json` and replayed without re-running the LLM.
  80: 
  81: ### Stage
  82: A named group of `Action`s within a `DevelopmentScript`. Stages reflect a natural development progression: `skeleton` → `types` → `impl (layer 0)` → `impl (layer 1)` → … → `tests` → `misc`. Files within a stage are ordered by dependency depth.
  83: 
  84: ### Action
  85: A single thing the engine does during playback. One of: `CreateFile`, `EditFile`, `MoveCursor`, `RunCommand`, `ThinkPause`, `DeleteFile`. Each action targets a specific file or performs a specific operation.
  86: 
  87: ### `Edit`
  88: A find-and-replace pair `{ find: String, replace: String }` used by `EditFile` and the final transition of `CreateFile`. Produced by the assembler from a diff between consecutive file versions. Applied using VSCode's find-and-replace dialog (Ctrl+H / Cmd+H).
  89: 
  90: ### Stub
  91: The initial version of a file: function and type signatures preserved exactly, all function bodies replaced with `todo!()`, all comments removed. Generated by the `GenerateStub` AI task. The first thing typed for any non-trivial file.
  92: 
  93: ### Intermediate version
  94: A whole-file version of a source file that is more complete than the stub but less complete than the final content. Intermediate versions bridge the gap between stub and final, making the typing progression look natural. The number of intermediates depends on file complexity: 0 (Trivial), 1 (Small), 2 (Medium), 3 (Large).
  95: 
  96: ### Final content
  97: The verbatim content of the file as it exists in the source repository. This is never modified — ghostwriter types existing code, it does not invent.
  98: 
  99: ### Complexity
 100: A four-level classification of a file by line count: `Trivial` (<30 lines), `Small` (30–100), `Medium` (100–300), `Large` (>300). Determines how many intermediate versions are generated.
 101: 
 102: ### File category
 103: The role of a file within the project, used for stage assignment: `Config`, `EntryPoint`, `Types`, `Impl`, `Tests`, `Misc`. Assigned by `analyzer.rs` using filename and content heuristics.
 104: 
 105: ### Dependency graph
 106: A directed graph where nodes are source files and edges represent `use crate::X` import relationships. Built by `analyzer.rs`. Used by `stage_planner.rs` to topologically sort files so that dependencies are always typed before the files that depend on them.
 107: 
 108: ### Topological sort
 109: The ordering of files such that no file appears before any of its dependencies. Computed using Kahn's algorithm in `stage_planner.rs`. Files at the same dependency depth are grouped into a single impl stage.
 110: 
 111: ### StagePlan
 112: The intermediate output of `stage_planner::plan()`: a list of `PlannedStage`s, each containing `FileSlot`s. Not yet a `DevelopmentScript` — it becomes one after AI tasks run and the assembler combines the results.
 113: 
 114: ### `AiTask`
 115: A unit of work sent to the Anthropic API. One of: `GenerateStub`, `GenerateIntermediates`, `DescribeTransition`. Each task targets a single file. Tasks are executed sequentially with stubs running before intermediates for the same file.
 116: 
 117: ### `AiResult`
 118: The parsed response to an `AiTask`: `Stub { content }`, `Intermediates { versions }`, or `Description { text }`.
 119: 
 120: ### Script cache
 121: The file `.ghostwriter/script.json` — a persisted `DevelopmentScript`. If this file exists (or is passed via `--script`), the orchestration step (clone analysis + all AI calls) is skipped entirely on subsequent runs.
 122: 
 123: ---
 124: 
 125: ## Playback / Engine
 126: 
 127: ### Playback
 128: The phase of ghostwriter's operation where the `DevelopmentScript` is executed — VSCode is open at the destination, and the engine types code character by character, navigates files, and runs commands as instructed.
 129: 
 130: ### Engine
 131: `typer/engine.rs`. The top-level loop that iterates stages and actions, dispatching each action to the appropriate handler (human simulator, editor commands, navigator).
 132: 
 133: ### Navigator
 134: `navigator/mod.rs` — the `Navigator` struct. Responsible for opening files in VSCode by visually finding their row in the Explorer sidebar and clicking it. Falls back to `code --goto` if visual navigation fails.
 135: 
 136: ### CLI fallback
 137: When the navigator fails to find a file's template (score below `match_threshold` after 3 attempts), it calls `vscode::open_file(abs_path)` which uses `code --goto` to open the file via the command line. The recording continues normally — the viewer typically cannot tell the difference.
 138: 
 139: ### Human simulator
 140: `typer/human.rs`. Generates per-character delays from a `LogNormal` distribution and probabilistically inserts typos. All randomness is seeded by `--seed` for reproducibility.
 141: 
 142: ### Typo event
 143: A simulated typing mistake. Four kinds: `AdjacentSub` (wrong adjacent key, 60% weight), `ExtraChar` (correct + extra key, 20%), `AccidentalEnter` (Enter pressed accidentally, 10%), `Transposition` (two chars swapped, 10%). Each is followed by a `notice_after` pause before correction.
 144: 
 145: ### `notice_after`
 146: The simulated delay (ms) between making a typo and noticing and correcting it. Sampled uniformly from a configurable range (default 300–1200ms). Represents the time taken to visually register the mistake.
 147: 
 148: ### Thinking pause
 149: A spontaneous pause inserted every N characters (N from `think_every_chars` config range). Represents a developer stopping to plan, re-read, or look something up. Duration sampled from `think_duration` config range (default 800–2500ms).
 150: 
 151: ### Bézier movement
 152: The quadratic Bézier curve used for all mouse movements. `P0` = current position, `P2` = target, `P1` = midpoint with a random offset of ±(distance × 0.3). Advances in 8ms steps. Makes cursor movement appear natural rather than teleporting.
 153: 
 154: ---
 155: 
 156: ## File System
 157: 
 158: ### Source root
 159: The `PathBuf` of the fully-cloned or locally-resolved repository that ghostwriter analyses and bootstraps against. Never written to during playback.
 160: 
 161: ### Destination
 162: The `PathBuf` of the empty directory where VSCode is open for playback. The engine creates files here character by character.
 163: 
 164: ### `.ghostwriter/`
 165: The working directory created inside the **source root** during bootstrap. Contains: `dictionary.json`, `templates/*.png`, `script.json` (if cached), `diagnostics/*.png` (if any bootstrap steps failed). Should be added to `.gitignore`.
 166: 
 167: ### Relative path
 168: A file path relative to the repo root (e.g. `src/main.rs`). Used as the key in `VisualDictionary` so that templates captured from the source root remain valid when used during playback at the destination root.

===== /docs/PLATFORMS.md =====

   1: # ghostwriter — Platform Support Matrix
   2: 
   3: **Last revised:** 2026-05
   4: 
   5: This document records what actually differs between macOS, Linux, and Windows for each subsystem. It is the authoritative reference before writing any platform-specific code. When in doubt, check here first.
   6: 
   7: ---
   8: 
   9: ## Status Summary
  10: 
  11: | Platform | Status | Notes |
  12: |---|---|---|
  13: | **macOS** | ✅ Primary | All subsystems implemented and tested. Development machine. |
  14: | **Linux (X11)** | ⚠️ Partial | Builds and mostly works. `wmctrl` dependency is non-standard. Wayland is blocked. |
  15: | **Linux (Wayland)** | ❌ Blocked | Screen capture via `screenshots` crate does not work without portal permissions. |
  16: | **Windows** | ⚠️ Skeleton | `focus()` and keyboard shortcuts are written but untested. `which_code()` is broken. |
  17: 
  18: ---
  19: 
  20: ## 1. Modifier Keys
  21: 
  22: The established pattern in `editor.rs` — use it everywhere keyboard shortcuts are constructed:
  23: 
  24: ```rust
  25: #[cfg(target_os = "macos")]
  26: const MOD: Key = Key::Meta;      // Cmd
  27: #[cfg(not(target_os = "macos"))]
  28: const MOD: Key = Key::Control;   // Ctrl
  29: ```
  30: 
  31: | Shortcut | macOS | Linux / Windows |
  32: |---|---|---|
  33: | Select all | `Cmd+A` | `Ctrl+A` |
  34: | Find & Replace | `Cmd+H` | `Ctrl+H` |
  35: | Go to line | `Cmd+G` | `Ctrl+G` |
  36: | Command palette | `Cmd+Shift+P` | `Ctrl+Shift+P` |
  37: | Open Explorer sidebar | `Cmd+Shift+E` | `Ctrl+Shift+E` |
  38: | New file | `Cmd+N` | `Ctrl+N` |
  39: | Toggle fullscreen | `Ctrl+Cmd+F` | `F11` |
  40: | Integrated terminal | `Ctrl+\`` | `Ctrl+\`` *(same on all)* |
  41: 
  42: **Note:** `open_terminal()` in `editor.rs` correctly uses `Key::Control` for the backtick shortcut on all platforms — VSCode uses Ctrl+\` universally, not the platform modifier.
  43: 
  44: **Note:** `Key::Meta` on Windows is the **Windows key**, not Ctrl. The `#[cfg(not(target_os = "macos"))]` branch correctly maps to `Key::Control` on Windows, so the pattern is sound — but verify with enigo's documentation if behaviour is unexpected.
  45: 
  46: ---
  47: 
  48: ## 2. Screen Capture
  49: 
  50: Uses the `screenshots` crate (`screenshots::Screen`).
  51: 
  52: | Aspect | macOS | Linux (X11) | Linux (Wayland) | Windows |
  53: |---|---|---|---|---|
  54: | Underlying API | ScreenCaptureKit (13+) / CGDisplayCreateImage | XCB / libX11 | ❌ Not supported | DXGI Desktop Duplication |
  55: | Permission required | Screen Recording (System Settings → Privacy) | None (X11 server access) | Portal permission required | None by default |
  56: | Retina / HiDPI | `capture_screen()` returns physical pixels at 2× | DPI scaling varies by compositor | N/A | Returns physical pixels at Windows DPI scale |
  57: | Black frame detection | Needed if permission denied | Not needed (X11 never returns black) | N/A | Not needed |
  58: 
  59: **macOS:** `capture_screen()` returns a `DynamicImage` at physical pixel resolution. On a Retina display this is 2× the logical window size. `scale_factor = 2.0` on most MacBook displays. `scale_down()` must be called before any coordinate is passed to `enigo`.
  60: 
  61: **Linux X11:** Physical and logical pixels are typically 1:1 on non-HiDPI displays. HiDPI Linux setups vary — the compositor may scale, giving a logical/physical ratio of 1.25, 1.5, or 2.0. `scale_factor` detection must be tested against real HiDPI Linux setups.
  62: 
  63: **Linux Wayland:** The `screenshots` crate cannot capture under Wayland without the XDG Desktop Portal. This requires `xdg-desktop-portal` and either `xdg-desktop-portal-gnome` or `xdg-desktop-portal-wlr` to be running. Until this is wired up, Wayland is unsupported. Users on Wayland should launch ghostwriter with `WAYLAND_DISPLAY= DISPLAY=:0` to force X11 fallback if XWayland is available.
  64: 
  65: **Windows:** Works without special permissions. Physical pixels at the Windows DPI scale factor. A 150% DPI display returns physical pixels at 1.5× logical resolution.
  66: 
  67: ---
  68: 
  69: ## 3. Window Focus (`vscode::focus()`)
  70: 
  71: Current implementation in `typer/vscode.rs`:
  72: 
  73: | Platform | Method | Reliability | Notes |
  74: |---|---|---|---|
  75: | macOS | `osascript -e 'tell application "Visual Studio Code" to activate'` | ✅ High | AppleScript. Reliable, native. Requires `osascript` crate (already in Cargo.toml as macOS-only dep). |
  76: | Linux | `wmctrl -a "Visual Studio Code"` | ⚠️ Medium | Requires `wmctrl` to be installed (`sudo apt install wmctrl`). Not available on Wayland. Not installed by default on most distros. |
  77: | Windows | `SetForegroundWindow` via PowerShell + P/Invoke | ⚠️ Unknown | Implemented but untested. `FindWindow` by title string is fragile if the VSCode window title includes the workspace name (e.g. "my-project — Visual Studio Code"). |
  78: 
  79: **Linux gap:** `wmctrl` is not installed by default. A preflight check should verify it exists before bootstrap begins, with a clear error message: `wmctrl is required on Linux. Install it with: sudo apt install wmctrl`. Add to `preflight_check()` in `bootstrap.rs`.
  80: 
  81: **Windows gap:** `FindWindow(NULL, "Visual Studio Code")` will fail when VSCode has a workspace open because the title becomes `"my-repo — Visual Studio Code"`. Use `FindWindowEx` with a partial match, or enumerate windows and filter by executable name instead.
  82: 
  83: ---
  84: 
  85: ## 4. VSCode CLI (`code`)
  86: 
  87: | Platform | Command | Common install paths | Notes |
  88: |---|---|---|---|
  89: | macOS | `code` | `/usr/local/bin/code`, `/opt/homebrew/bin/code` | Installed via VSCode → Command Palette → "Install 'code' command in PATH" |
  90: | Linux | `code`, `code-oss`, `codium` | `/usr/bin/code`, `/snap/bin/code`, `/usr/local/bin/code` | Varies by install method. Snap installs may have sandboxing issues with `--goto`. |
  91: | Windows | `code.cmd` or `code` | `%APPDATA%\Local\Programs\Microsoft VS Code\bin\` | Must be on `%PATH%`. Installer offers to add it. |
  92: 
  93: **Current bug in `which_code()` (Windows):** The function calls `which code` using Unix `which`. This doesn't exist on Windows. Fix:
  94: 
  95: ```rust
  96: fn which_code() -> Result<()> {
  97:     #[cfg(target_os = "windows")]
  98:     let cmd = Command::new("where").arg("code");
  99:     #[cfg(not(target_os = "windows"))]
 100:     let cmd = Command::new("which").arg("code");
 101:     
 102:     cmd.output()
 103:         .map_err(|_| GhostError::VscodeNotFound)?
 104:         .status
 105:         .success()
 106:         .then_some(())
 107:         .ok_or(GhostError::VscodeNotFound.into())
 108: }
 109: ```
 110: 
 111: **Linux Snap installs:** VSCode installed via Snap may restrict the `--goto` flag from working correctly due to filesystem sandboxing. Test with both Snap and `.deb` installs. Consider documenting this limitation.
 112: 
 113: ---
 114: 
 115: ## 5. Fullscreen Detection and Toggling
 116: 
 117: Used in bootstrap Step 3 (`force_fullscreen()`).
 118: 
 119: | Platform | Toggle shortcut | Detection method | Notes |
 120: |---|---|---|---|
 121: | macOS | `Ctrl+Cmd+F` | Luma heuristic: `abs(top_luma - body_luma) < 35` | Works reliably. The macOS menu bar disappears in fullscreen, changing the top luma. |
 122: | Linux | `F11` | Same luma heuristic | May fail with tiling window managers (i3, sway, Hyprland) that don't have a traditional fullscreen. The luma heuristic still works if decorations disappear. |
 123: | Windows | `F11` | Same luma heuristic | Untested. Should work — VSCode title bar disappears in fullscreen, changing top luma. |
 124: 
 125: The fullscreen shortcut is currently hardcoded per platform in `bootstrap.rs`. Verify the key constants:
 126: 
 127: ```rust
 128: #[cfg(target_os = "macos")]
 129: fn fullscreen_key() -> (Key, Key) { (Key::Control, Key::Meta) } // Ctrl+Cmd+F
 130: #[cfg(not(target_os = "macos"))]
 131: fn fullscreen_key() -> Key { Key::F11 }
 132: ```
 133: 
 134: **Tiling WM gap (Linux):** On tiling window managers, VSCode may be maximised-but-not-fullscreen, or truly fullscreen but without the luma difference that the heuristic detects. The heuristic may need to be extended with a window geometry check on Linux. Consider using `xdotool getactivewindow getwindowgeometry` to confirm the window fills the display.
 135: 
 136: ---
 137: 
 138: ## 6. Input Injection (enigo)
 139: 
 140: | Aspect | macOS | Linux (X11) | Linux (Wayland) | Windows |
 141: |---|---|---|---|---|
 142: | Permission | Accessibility (System Settings → Privacy) | None | `ydotool` or `dotool` required | None |
 143: | Unicode text input | `enigo.text("…")` | `enigo.text("…")` via XSendEvent | Blocked without `ydotool` | `enigo.text("…")` via SendInput |
 144: | Key chords | `enigo.key(Key::…, Down/Up)` | Same | Same issue | Same |
 145: 
 146: **macOS:** Accessibility permission must be granted to Terminal (or the ghostwriter binary itself if distributed as an app). The preflight check in `bootstrap.rs` detects this by attempting a `Key::Shift` press and catching the error.
 147: 
 148: **Linux Wayland:** Direct input injection is blocked by the Wayland security model. `ydotool` provides a workaround but requires a running `ydotoold` daemon and root privileges to install the uinput device. Not practical as a silent dependency. **Wayland users must use XWayland.**
 149: 
 150: ---
 151: 
 152: ## 7. DPI / Scale Factor Detection
 153: 
 154: Current approach: `screenshots` crate returns image dimensions; `scale_factor` is computed by comparing the captured image size against the known logical display size from OS APIs.
 155: 
 156: | Platform | How scale_factor is detected | Common values |
 157: |---|---|---|
 158: | macOS | `screenshots::Screen::all()` returns physical pixel dimensions; divide by `NSScreen.frame` logical size | 2.0 (Retina), 1.0 (external non-Retina monitor) |
 159: | Linux X11 | `Xft.dpi` resource, or `/sys/class/drm/*/edid` parsing | 1.0 (standard), 1.25, 1.5, 2.0 (HiDPI) |
 160: | Windows | `GetDpiForSystem()` / `GetDpiForMonitor()` | 1.0 (96 dpi), 1.25 (120 dpi), 1.5 (144 dpi), 2.0 (192 dpi) |
 161: 
 162: **The invariant that must hold on all platforms:**
 163: ```
 164: scale_factor = physical_pixel_dimension / logical_pixel_dimension
 165: enigo_coordinate = screenshot_coordinate / scale_factor
 166: ```
 167: 
 168: Any platform code that touches coordinates must respect this. See CLAUDE.md invariants.
 169: 
 170: ---
 171: 
 172: ## 8. What Needs Doing Per Platform
 173: 
 174: ### macOS (Primary)
 175: - No gaps in current implementation
 176: - Fullscreen, screen capture, input injection, window focus all work
 177: - HiDPI scale_factor detection is reliable
 178: 
 179: ### Linux (to reach parity)
 180: 
 181: | Item | Effort | Priority |
 182: |---|---|---|
 183: | Add `wmctrl` preflight check with clear error message | Small | High |
 184: | Fix `which_code()` to handle `code-oss` and `codium` variants | Small | High |
 185: | Test HiDPI scale_factor detection on a real HiDPI Linux display | Medium | Medium |
 186: | Test with tiling WM (i3, sway) — fullscreen detection may need adjustment | Medium | Medium |
 187: | Document Wayland limitation in README and bootstrap error | Small | High |
 188: | Investigate `ydotool` as Wayland input fallback | Large | Low |
 189: 
 190: ### Windows (to reach parity)
 191: 
 192: | Item | Effort | Priority |
 193: |---|---|---|
 194: | Fix `which_code()` to use `where` instead of `which` | Trivial | Critical |
 195: | Fix `FindWindow` in `focus()` to handle workspace-titled windows | Small | High |
 196: | Test keyboard shortcut constants (`Ctrl` vs platform variations) | Small | High |
 197: | Test screen capture and scale_factor on a HiDPI Windows display | Medium | Medium |
 198: | Test fullscreen toggle (F11) and luma detection | Small | High |
 199: | Verify `enigo` Unicode text input works correctly | Small | High |
 200: 
 201: ---
 202: 
 203: ## 9. Testing Cross-Platform Code
 204: 
 205: Until CI exists for each platform, the safest approach:
 206: 
 207: - Write all platform branches at the same time, even if only macOS is tested
 208: - Mark untested platform branches with `// TODO: untested on <platform>` comments
 209: - Keep platform-specific code isolated in `vscode.rs` and `editor.rs` — the rest of the codebase should be platform-agnostic
 210: - The `cfg(not(target_os = "macos"))` pattern covers both Linux and Windows — that's intentional for now, but will need splitting when Windows-specific fixes are needed
